I'm thinking about adding 週に一度クラスメイトを買う話 ～ふたりの時間、言い訳の五千円～ to this project as an analytic target. Based on what you know about the story, does it fill in any gaps?

**Assistant — Turn 2**

## Verdict

**Yes—quite strongly.** *週に一度クラスメイトを買う話* would fill a genuine gap rather than merely adding another school romance or another yuri-adjacent relationship to the corpus.

Its distinctive contribution would be **sustained dyadic micropsychology**: two girls constructing an increasingly intimate relationship through money, commands, rules, excuses, bodily boundaries, and stubborn refusal to name what they are doing. KADOKAWA’s premise already establishes the central contradiction: Miyagi pays Sendai ¥5,000 for the right to issue commands, yet both insist they are neither friends nor lovers even as the arrangement becomes emotionally indispensable. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.kadokawa.co.jp&sz=128)KADOKAWAオフィシャルサイト+1](https://www.kadokawa.co.jp/product/322210001354/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### The largest gap it fills: a relationship with almost nowhere to hide

Much of the current project is built around **ensembles and institutions**:

-   bands, orchestras, idol units, schools, agencies, military organizations;

-   characters locating themselves inside inherited traditions;

-   private wounds becoming public performance;

-   relationships mediated by group membership, professional duty, or political structure.


Even the most intimate existing works generally retain large social architectures. *Sound! Euphonium* places desire and intimacy inside the ensemble; *Girls Band Cry* converts interpersonal contradiction into collective music; *IDOLY PRIDE* distributes grief and inheritance through an entire professional ecosystem.[

Sound Euphonium Full Series Ana…

](/g/g-p-69ab0e4e06188191bcc4c71aea012ad8/c/6a67993f-6864-83ea-a19f-f988c4a61a82?messageId=e66754b0-81e4-4a0d-824a-b201ef98dd18&conversation_save=1)[

Girls Band Cry synthesis

](/g/g-p-69ab0e4e06188191bcc4c71aea012ad8/c/6a67cda0-0f4c-83ea-aa93-8fb8df25a43b?messageId=b7d98cd7-74c1-4b83-b9dc-76338ccdb82e&conversation_save=1)IDOLY PRIDE Analysis Setup

*週クラ* strips most of that away.

Its fundamental unit is:

> two girls, one room, three hours, five thousand yen, and a rule neither wants to examine too closely.

That creates a form of analytical pressure largely absent elsewhere in the project. There is no ensemble chorus to explain them, no competition arc to externalize development, and no supernatural mechanism to embody their contradictions. Minute changes in wording, routine, permission, physical distance, food, clothing, touch, jealousy, and domestic behavior become the plot.

Author Haneda Usa has explicitly described it as a novel written almost exclusively about these two characters. She also emphasizes realism, alternating first-person narration, and the need for Miyagi’s and Sendai’s thoughts and speech to remain unmistakably distinct. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://mequrimequru.jp&sz=128)Mequri Mequru](https://mequrimequru.jp/articles/details/1267251/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

That makes it unusually compatible with the kind of voice-centered, psychologically exact reading we have done for *Monogatari*, *Oregairu*, and *Oreimo*—but in a much narrower and more sustained relational chamber.

## What it would add

### 1\. Canonical female–female romance as the central process

The project already contains many intense, exclusive, or yuri-coded female relationships. But those are often:

-   unresolved by genre design;

-   subordinate to an idol, band, or school narrative;

-   one relationship among many;

-   or left deliberately suspended between friendship, artistic partnership, devotion, and romance.


*週クラ* is different. Romantic and sexual attachment is not a possible interpretation layered onto another narrative. **The difficulty of becoming capable of acknowledging that attachment is the narrative.**

The ambiguity still matters, but it is not “are they romantically interested?” ambiguity. It is:

> What vocabulary, structure, and obligations could contain what they already feel?

That would make it a useful control case when discussing supposedly romantic ambiguity elsewhere. It demonstrates the difference between **textual indeterminacy** and **characters who know less—or admit less—than the text does**.

### 2\. Contractual intimacy and the use of power as an emotional prosthesis

The ¥5,000 arrangement is analytically rich because the money does several incompatible things at once.

It creates a real asymmetry: Miyagi purchases authority and Sendai agrees to obey.

It also creates protection. Neither girl has to say:

-   I wanted to see you.

-   I need you to stay.

-   I am jealous.

-   Touch me.

-   Let me touch you.

-   Choose me over the life you display to everyone else.


They can say instead:

> I paid you.
> It was an order.
> Those are the rules.

The contract gives desire an external alibi.

That means the work is not merely about dominance and submission. It is about **formal structures invented by emotionally avoidant people so that intimacy can occur without being confessed**. Commands become displaced requests; obedience can become chosen closeness; rule-breaking reveals desire; money both commodifies time and marks it as uniquely reserved.

This would complement *Oreimo*’s treatment of hidden selves and confession through media, and *Monogatari*’s interest in roles, verbal games, bodily boundaries, and stories people construct to survive themselves.[

Oreimo Full Series Synthesis

](/g/g-p-69ab0e4e06188191bcc4c71aea012ad8/c/6a68050b-b808-83ea-8f1f-064aabe99c2d?messageId=b7645efe-4e7f-47d0-abca-538f0e9a8541&conversation_save=1)Monogatari Series Analysis

It would also let us examine consent with more precision than a simple healthy/unhealthy binary. The questions would include:

-   When is a technically permitted command still emotionally coercive?

-   When does refusal become possible?

-   Does Sendai’s willingness to obey give her a different kind of power?

-   When are the rules containing danger, and when are they enabling avoidance?

-   What happens once an arrangement created as an excuse becomes an obligation neither can abandon?


### 3\. Alternating unreliability inside the same relationship

The alternating first-person structure may be the work’s greatest formal value.

Both girls repeatedly interpret the same actions differently. Each has access to information the other lacks, while remaining strikingly ignorant about her own motives. The reader is therefore not solving a mystery in the normal sense. The reader watches two partial consciousnesses circle a reality that becomes obvious in the space between them.

Miyagi tends to bury need beneath irritation, possession, tests, and accumulating excuses. Sendai is socially adaptive and initially appears more self-aware, but her ability to accommodate others also obscures how fixated, possessive, and emotionally dependent she is becoming. Haneda herself characterizes Miyagi through layered excuses and refusal to be honest, while describing Sendai’s increasingly excessive preoccupation with Miyagi as both troublesome and central to her appeal. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://mequrimequru.jp&sz=128)Mequri Mequru](https://mequrimequru.jp/articles/details/1267251/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

This gives the project something it currently has only intermittently: **a long experiment in comparative interiority**, where nearly every important event can be reconstructed through two incompatible but mutually illuminating minds.

### 4\. Private domesticity rather than public performance

The corpus is unusually rich in characters becoming visible:

-   idols on stages;

-   musicians before audiences;

-   heroes under public judgment;

-   soldiers inside history;

-   students establishing legitimacy inside institutions.


*週クラ* moves in the opposite direction. Its central question is what people become **when they are removed from the social audience**.

Sendai’s socially fluent school persona and Miyagi’s lower-status, withdrawn identity matter, but the room permits a counter-society. Inside it, their public ranking can be inverted. Miyagi commands the socially dominant girl. Sendai can abandon the personality she uses to move smoothly through school. Yet the private room is not automatically authentic; it produces its own roles and evasions.

That would add a useful distinction to the project:

> Public performance can conceal the self, but privacy does not automatically reveal it.
> Two people can construct an equally elaborate private performance together.

### 5\. A relationship surviving the end of school

This may be the most important long-term contribution.

The published series does not stop at graduation. Volumes 1–4 form the high-school movement; from Volume 5 onward, the story follows Miyagi and Sendai into university life and living under the same roof. The progression therefore tests whether a relationship originally sustained by a weekly payment, classroom proximity, and Miyagi’s room can survive after those conditions disappear. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://mequrimequru.jp&sz=128)Mequri Mequru+1](https://mequrimequru.jp/articles/details/1267251/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

That fills a recurring gap in school-centered anime and light novels: many works treat graduation as emotional culmination, leaving adult continuity hypothetical. *週クラ* asks what happens when exceptional, secret meetings must become ordinary life:

-   shared space;

-   birthdays;

-   outings;

-   separate university networks;

-   domestic routines;

-   jealousy toward lives not fully shared;

-   and the need to make “special” behavior sustainable.


The later official descriptions emphasize precisely this movement: previously exceptional acts become intended everyday practices, while the latest published volume remains concerned with the disturbing fact that each girl still possesses parts of herself unknown to the other. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://store.kadokawa.co.jp&sz=128)Kadokawa Store+1](https://store.kadokawa.co.jp/shop/g/g322502001768/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Where it overlaps rather than fills gaps

Its closest existing neighbors would probably be:

-   **Oregairu** — indirectness, social masks, aversion to naming desire, and relationships built through elaborate verbal self-protection.

-   **Oreimo** — recognition of divided selves and intimacy created through a secret unavailable to ordinary social life.

-   **Monogatari** — self-deceptive narration, verbalized power games, bodily boundaries, and desire displaced into strange rituals.

-   **Girls Band Cry** — abrasive people whose sincerity does not make them easy or ethically uncomplicated.

-   Some of the more exclusive *IDOLY PRIDE* and *Gakumas* pairs—especially those where care, rivalry, possession, and identity become difficult to separate.


But it differs by removing most of those works’ larger machinery. It is almost a laboratory version of several themes already important to the project.

## The main reservation

It may not justify the exact same volume-by-volume format used for *86*, *Youjo Senki*, or *Sound! Euphonium*.

The story’s development is cumulative, repetitive by design, and concentrated in tiny behavioral changes. That repetition is meaningful—it shows routines acquiring new emotional significance—but an analysis that treats every volume as a new thematic architecture could artificially inflate minor movement.

A better structure would be:

1.  **Volumes 1–4: the high-school contract**, analyzed individually or in two-volume movements.

2.  **A high-school synthesis** focused on money, commands, class position, the room, touch, names, and graduation.

3.  **Volumes 5 onward: cohabitation and ordinary continuity**, with somewhat more compact volume analyses.

4.  **A longitudinal relationship synthesis** once the published arc reaches a suitable stopping point.


The work is still ongoing. As of August 2026, nine Japanese volumes have been published, the web version continues beyond 400 installments, and the author’s Kakuyomu page indicates Volume 10 is planned for autumn 2026. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.kadokawa.co.jp&sz=128)KADOKAWAオフィシャルサイト+1](https://www.kadokawa.co.jp/product/322511000827/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Recommendation

I would rank it as a **high-value addition**, especially for these comparative categories:

> female interiority;
> canonical yuri romance;
> unreliable alternating narration;
> transactional and ritualized intimacy;
> consent and boundary negotiation;
> private versus public selves;
> domestic cohabitation;
> emotional avoidance;
> possessiveness without overt melodrama;
> and the conversion of exceptional meetings into ordinary shared life.

The original Japanese novels should be the primary analytical source. The author’s deliberate differentiation of the two first-person voices means translation would preserve the plot and broad psychology but could flatten precisely the register, repetition, thought rhythms, forms of address, and evasive wording that make the work analytically distinctive. The manga and voice dramas would be useful later as adaptation and performance supplements, not substitutes for the prose.

So: **it fills one of the clearest remaining gaps in the project—a prolonged, canonical, female–female relationship novel in which almost the entire dramatic world is the changing distance between two people.**

**User — Turn 3**

I think one of the interesting things I've read online (which you may or may not be able to corroborate) is that Sendai - despite being the one being paid - is the more sexually adventurous of the two and is more naturally suited to being an initiator

---

**Assistant — Turn 4**

**Yes, that reading is strongly supported**, with one qualification: Sendai seems **more sexually proactive and exploratory**, not necessarily more sexually experienced. I did not find an author statement explicitly assigning them fixed initiator/receptive roles, but the text repeatedly establishes the pattern itself.

The apparent paradox is central to the relationship:

> **Miyagi possesses the formal authority. Sendai possesses much of the practical initiative.**

Miyagi pays, gives commands, establishes rules, and decides whether Sendai may enter her room. Yet Sendai is generally more comfortable turning ambiguous physical intimacy into an action, asking for continuation, or testing whether Miyagi will permit escalation.

## Miyagi creates the structure; Sendai exploits its possibilities

The initial ¥5,000 arrangement gives Miyagi an artificial instrument for acting on impulses she could never express as ordinary desire. In the opening chapter, Miyagi uses the contract to order Sendai across an intimate boundary specifically because she wants to test whether Sendai will refuse. Sendai hesitates but complies. The official volume description similarly emphasizes that Miyagi sometimes gives “dangerous” commands and continually tests a girl who supposedly refuses nothing. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)Kakuyomu+1](https://kakuyomu.jp/works/1177354054894027232/episodes/1177354054894027298?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

So Miyagi is not sexually passive in the ordinary sense. She originates some of their most transgressive situations. But she generally does so through **displacement**:

-   It is an order.

-   I paid you.

-   I am only testing you.

-   The specific act does not matter.

-   I want proof that you obey me.


The contract lets her convert desire into authority without admitting it is desire.

Sendai needs that alibi less. Once an intimate possibility exists, she is more likely to engage with it as something she actually wants.

A later cohabitation chapter makes the distinction unusually clear. Miyagi approaches Sendai physically, then stops herself. Sendai asks for the continuation, argues that it should take priority over breakfast, and initiates the kiss herself when Miyagi retreats. She then tries to extend it while Miyagi reasserts the mundane boundary: the food is getting cold. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)Kakuyomu](https://kakuyomu.jp/works/1177354054894027232/episodes/16818792438147210428)

That is a recurring relational rhythm:

> **Miyagi opens the door impulsively.
> Sendai walks through it deliberately.
> Miyagi then panics about how far they have gone.**

## Sendai is more comfortable converting desire into behavior

Sendai’s internal narration is also frequently more candid about physical desire, even when she remains evasive about love and relational status.

In one later university-era scene, Miyagi initiates possessive marking. Sendai not only cooperates without hesitation; internally, she imagines removing the remaining barriers herself and taking the initiative if Miyagi refuses to continue. The moment is interrupted, and Sendai immediately asks whether they can resume afterward. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)Kakuyomu](https://kakuyomu.jp/works/1177354054894027232/episodes/16817330648935399607?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

That is stronger than mere obedience. She is not simply allowing Miyagi to direct events. She is:

-   anticipating further intimacy;

-   welcoming escalation;

-   imagining reversing the active/passive arrangement;

-   and regarding interruption as postponement rather than conclusion.


Sendai also tends to use teasing, requests, touch, praise, and strategic persistence to pull Miyagi forward. Because she is socially fluent, she can make initiative look casual. She can ask “and the rest?” or move closer with an air of playfulness, preserving plausible deniability while still exerting pressure.

Miyagi’s desire is often more intense but less usable. It appears as:

-   jealousy;

-   ownership;

-   wanting to leave marks;

-   commands;

-   irritation at Sendai’s other relationships;

-   fixation on whether Sendai belongs to her;

-   and sudden physical acts followed by immediate retreat.


Sendai’s desire is more readily translated into **pursuit**.

## Their real power division

Reducing them to dominant Miyagi and submissive Sendai would miss most of what the novel is doing.

A more accurate division is:

The compact formulation would be:

> **Miyagi controls permission; Sendai generates momentum.**

Or, slightly more psychologically:

> **Miyagi is better suited to possession. Sendai is better suited to pursuit.**

That difference explains why the financial hierarchy does not determine the erotic hierarchy. Sendai’s obedience is partly chosen and sometimes actively provocative. She discovers that being “ordered” gives her permission to participate, but once participation becomes available, she is often the one more willing to find out where it leads.

## Why Sendai is naturally better suited to initiating

Several aspects of her personality converge here.

**She is more socially adaptive.** Sendai reads moods, modulates her presentation, tests reactions, and knows how to recover from awkwardness. Initiative is less catastrophic for someone who trusts her ability to manage another person’s response.

**She is more comfortable with embodied affection.** Miyagi experiences touch as dangerous evidence of dependency. Sendai increasingly experiences it as one of the few reliable ways to get past Miyagi’s verbal defenses.

**She wants reciprocity.** Mere obedience eventually becomes insufficient. Sendai wants Miyagi to touch her because Miyagi desires it, not merely because the contract permits it. Her escalating initiative often tries to force that distinction into visibility.

**She has higher tolerance for ambiguity.** Miyagi responds to undefined intimacy by inventing rules. Sendai can remain inside the undefined state while continuing to act.

**Her frustration becomes pursuit.** When Miyagi refuses to speak, define, promise, or acknowledge, Sendai tries to extract an answer through proximity and behavior.

There is nevertheless a limit to calling her “adventurous.” Sendai remains emotionally cautious. She can initiate a kiss more easily than define the relationship created by it. Physical boldness and emotional courage are not identical. In some respects, her physical initiative becomes **her own form of avoidance**: she can pursue Miyagi bodily while postponing the verbal demand that would expose her to unequivocal rejection.

So I would corroborate the online characterization, but sharpen it:

> **Sendai is the more naturally erotic initiator because she can act inside ambiguity. Miyagi has equally strong—and sometimes more possessive—desire, but usually needs commands, rules, jealousy, or sudden impulses to make that desire actionable.**

That inversion is one of the strongest reasons the payment premise has analytical value. The person being “bought” gradually becomes the more active pursuer, while the ostensible buyer discovers that purchasing authority cannot give her control over what she feels.

[![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)Kakuyomu](https://kakuyomu.jp/works/1177354054894027232/episodes/1177354054894027298?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

-   **The full web serialization:** the extensive 425-chapter version written and maintained by Haneda Usa.

-   **The Fantasia Bunko expanded preview:** a much shorter promotional selection drawn from the commercially published light novels. Its page warns that the displayed text was prepared during production and may differ in places from the final books. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)Kakuyomu](https://kakuyomu.jp/works/16817330667602002457?utm_source=chatgpt.com)


That distinction matters analytically. The full Kakuyomu version gives us the complete developing narrative, but it is **the web-novel text**, not necessarily a perfect substitute for the published light novels. The print edition may contain revisions, rearranged passages, tightened prose, added material, or other editorial changes. Therefore:

> **Kakuyomu is sufficient for a serious complete-story reading.
> The Japanese light novels remain preferable as the definitive textual basis for volume-specific literary analysis.**

I can nevertheless read the web novel sequentially, search its chapter structure, compare Miyagi and Sendai’s alternating narration, and retrieve exact scenes relevant to questions such as initiative, commands, jealousy, touch, names, cohabitation, or sexual and emotional escalation. The practical limitation is scale: 425 separate webpages are less reliable and efficient for a comprehensive close reading than having the Japanese text consolidated into EPUB or plaintext. For targeted investigation or an initial full-story mapping, the official online version is entirely usable.

**User — Turn 7**

How is the web novel going to be different from the LN? Which is considered more "canon?"

---

**Assistant — Turn 8**

The cleanest way to think about it is **not “canon versus non-canon,” but “original serialization versus revised publication.”**

## How the web novel differs from the light novel

The Kakuyomu version is the original, continuously updated serialization. It currently runs to 425 installments and remains ongoing. The light novels reorganize that material into discrete volumes: Volumes 1–4 comprise the high-school movement, while Volume 5 begins the university/cohabitation movement. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)Kakuyomu+1](https://kakuyomu.jp/works/1177354054894027232/episodes/1177354054894027298?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

The light novels are **not simply the web pages pasted into books**. The author explicitly advertises published volumes as containing:

-   **加筆** — added writing;

-   **修正** — revisions;

-   **書き下ろし** — newly written material not previously serialized.


For example, Haneda’s Kakuyomu notice for Volume 8 states that it includes revisions, additions, and newly written material. In her interview, she also identifies the newly written extra episode at the end of Volume 4 as a bridge into the new life beginning in Volume 5. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)Kakuyomu+1](https://kakuyomu.jp/works/1177354054894027232/episodes/822139842012912834?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

That means the likely differences include:

### Revised prose

Sentences, internal monologue, dialogue rhythm, and descriptions can be rewritten or tightened. This matters unusually much for *週クラ*, because Haneda says she repeatedly revises the alternating first-person openings to ensure Miyagi always sounds like Miyagi and Sendai always sounds like Sendai. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://mequrimequru.jp&sz=128)Mequri Mequru](https://mequrimequru.jp/articles/details/1267251/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

The LN may therefore sharpen:

-   distinctions between their narrative voices;

-   the exact emotional implication of a line;

-   continuity between scenes;

-   repetitions inherited from serial publication;

-   and the pacing of gradual escalation.


### Structural reorganization

A web serial is designed around frequent individual updates. A printed novel needs a volume-length dramatic arc.

The LN can combine, divide, reorder, or bridge web installments so that each book has:

-   an opening state;

-   a central relational movement;

-   an ending point;

-   and stronger continuity into the next volume.


I have not yet performed a page-by-page collation, so I would not claim that major events have been radically rearranged. But structural editing is inherent in converting hundreds of short serialized installments into conventional 300–350-page books, and the documented additions and revisions confirm that some adaptation work occurred. The first published volume, for example, is a 342-page book, whereas the Kakuyomu version is divided into individually posted episodes of roughly a few thousand Japanese characters each. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)Kakuyomu+1](https://kakuyomu.jp/works/1177354054894027232/episodes/1177354054894027298)

### New scenes and supplementary material

The LN contains newly written episodes that may:

-   expand domestic transitions;

-   give additional perspective on a significant event;

-   connect two major phases of the relationship;

-   or show ordinary moments omitted from the original serial.


There are also retailer-exclusive short stories and author-written voice dramas, although I would treat those as supplementary paratext unless their relationship to the main volumes is clear. The ordinary LN’s own newly written chapters have stronger textual standing than a store-exclusive bonus. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.melonbooks.co.jp&sz=128)Melonbooks+1](https://www.melonbooks.co.jp/products/detail.php?product_id=3616585&utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### Illustrations and editorial framing

U35’s illustrations establish official visual interpretations of:

-   Miyagi and Sendai;

-   clothing and appearance changes;

-   bodily distance;

-   room layouts;

-   significant moments selected for emphasis.


They do not supersede the prose, but they become part of the published work’s interpretive apparatus. Volume titles, summaries, chapter grouping, afterwords, and promotional voice casting also give the commercial edition a more fixed identity than the continuously evolving website.

## Which one is more canonical?

I found **no public statement from Haneda or KADOKAWA declaring that the web novel is non-canon, or formally announcing a hierarchy between the two versions**.

So the strongest defensible answer is:

> **Both are legitimate author-written versions of the same story.
> Where they differ, the light novel should generally be treated as the definitive edition for material it has already adapted.**

“Definitive” is more accurate here than “the only canon.”

The LN has several claims to priority:

1.  It is the text revised by the author after the initial serialization.

2.  It incorporates editorial work and newly written connective material.

3.  It is the numbered, commercially published edition around which the franchise’s illustrations, marketing, manga, and voice projects are organized.

4.  It gives the author an opportunity to reconsider wording and structure with hindsight.


The web novel nevertheless remains important because:

1.  It is also written and maintained by Haneda.

2.  It contains the story’s original form.

3.  It is considerably ahead of the published books.

4.  It may preserve scenes, repetitions, or nuances changed or compressed in publication.

5.  It reveals how the relationship developed as serial fiction rather than as retrospectively shaped volumes.


The right rule would therefore be:

### When both versions cover the same event

**Use the LN as the primary text.**

If a line, motivation, sequence, or factual detail conflicts, the later published revision should normally control an LN-based character or thematic analysis.

### When the LN has not yet reached a web-novel event

**Use the web novel, but label the conclusion “web-novel continuity” or “WN-only as of Volume 9.”**

That material is not unofficial or fan-created. But it remains potentially subject to revision when adapted into a later volume.

### When the web novel contains something omitted from the LN

Do not automatically assume the event has been erased from continuity. An omission can mean:

-   deliberate removal;

-   compression for space;

-   postponement;

-   or simply that the printed version no longer needs the scene.


Its status has to be judged case by case. A direct contradiction carries more interpretive weight than silence.

## Recommended source hierarchy for this project

For a rigorous *週クラ* deep dive, I would use:

1.  **Japanese light novels as the core literary text**

2.  **Kakuyomu web novel as a parallel source and future-story continuation**

3.  **LN-exclusive and retailer short stories as supplementary character evidence**

4.  **Author-written voice dramas as performance supplements**

5.  **Manga as an adaptation with its own visual interpretation**


The ideal process would be to read each LN volume first, then identify its approximate Kakuyomu chapter range and run a targeted comparison:

-   what was rewritten;

-   what was added;

-   what disappeared;

-   whether a perspective chapter moved;

-   whether Miyagi or Sendai became more explicit;

-   and whether the LN alters the ethical or erotic implications of a scene.


For this particular work, that comparison could be unusually fruitful. Tiny changes in whether Sendai says something directly, merely thinks it, or is described acting before thinking can materially change our understanding of who is pursuing whom. Likewise, a small alteration in Miyagi’s internal justification can distinguish command, jealousy, fear, possession, and desire.

So my practical judgment is:

> **The LN is the authoritative edition for close literary analysis.
> The web novel is the authoritative record of the ongoing story beyond the LN and an indispensable comparative witness—not a discarded draft.**

**Assistant — Turn 10**

**Yes. Japanese fans noticed the overlap almost immediately.** The clearest searchable evidence is a 5ch thread whose title literally describes the *週クラ* PV cast as **「Lynn×市ノ瀬加那の水星コンビ」**—“the *Witch from Mercury* duo of Lynn and Kana Ichinose.” [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://greta.5ch.net&sz=128)Greta](https://greta.5ch.net/test/read.cgi/poverty/1766313725/l50)

The correspondence is exact:

-   **市ノ瀬加那 / Kana Ichinose**: Suletta Mercury → Sendai Hazuki

-   **Lynn**: Miorine Rembran → Miyagi Shiori


The official *Witch from Mercury* site confirms Ichinose as Suletta and Lynn as Miorine, while the *週クラ* promotional videos conspicuously headline the pairing as **「Lynn×市ノ瀬加那」** and identify them as Miyagi and Sendai. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://gundam-official.com&sz=128)Gundam Official+1](https://gundam-official.com/witch-from-mercury/news/detail.php?id=19928&utm_source=chatgpt.com)

The fan joke was therefore essentially:

> **SuleMio have been reincarnated into another school yuri story, except now there are no mobile suits and Miorine pays Suletta ¥5,000 a week.**

That wording is my summary of the joke rather than a direct quotation, but “水星コンビ” was demonstrably being used as the shorthand. The timing made the association especially obvious: the first *週クラ* voiced promotion appeared in June 2023, while *The Witch from Mercury*’s final cour was still airing. The pairing was at peak public recognizability.

There is also a mildly amusing role continuity:

-   Lynn again voices the **pricklier, more controlling, rule-making girl**.

-   Ichinose again voices the girl who is more accommodating on the surface but increasingly refuses to leave the other one alone.


It is not a perfect Miorine–Suletta mapping. Sendai is far more socially polished and erotically proactive than Suletta, while Miyagi is more withdrawn and self-isolating than Miorine. But the broad emotional silhouette—**Lynn’s defensive, possessive girl paired with Ichinose’s persistent pursuer**—made the casting feel almost uncannily appropriate.

The official marketing never explicitly said, “These are the SuleMio actresses together again,” at least in the material I found. Still, repeatedly putting **Lynn×市ノ瀬加那** at the front of PV titles rather than merely listing them in the description strongly suggests that their established chemistry and immediate recognizability were part of the appeal. That last point is an inference, not a confirmed casting rationale. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.youtube.com&sz=128)YouTube+1](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=G2t8j0EwohA&utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Why I would not start with the web novel

The WN is enormously useful, but I think using it in parallel during the first LN read would actually **degrade the literary analysis**.

The printed books are revised texts. Haneda repeatedly describes them as containing **加筆修正** (additions/revisions) and **書き下ろし** (newly written material). Volume 8, for example, was substantially expanded and reorganizes its extra material into paired Miyagi/Sendai perspectives; Volume 9 does something similar. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)カクヨム - 「書ける、読める、伝えられる」新しいWeb小説サイト+1](https://kakuyomu.jp/users/hanedausa/news/822139840843116397?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

So I want to understand:

> **What does Volume 4, as Volume 4, say?**

before asking:

> How did Haneda transform the corresponding Kakuyomu serialization into Volume 4?

Otherwise we risk unconsciously importing WN formulations into the LN and obscuring the author's revisions.

I would do the WN comparison **after each major literary movement**, especially after Volume 4, because that closes the high-school phase, and again after Volume 9.

# How much do we actually lose without the missing extras?

Less than the sheer number of missing stories makes it sound like.

For **core literary analysis**, I would characterize the loss as **small**.

For **fine-grained character analysis**, it becomes **noticeable but not crippling**.

For **archival completeness**, obviously it is substantial.

The important distinction is what kind of information those stories contain.

## Core narrative: almost nothing essential is lost

I have found no evidence that Haneda expects a reader to purchase six different retailer editions in order to follow the plot. Quite the opposite: she explicitly says of the Volume 9 retailer stories that they can each be enjoyed independently. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)カクヨム - 「書ける、読める、伝えられる」新しいWeb小説サイト](https://kakuyomu.jp/users/hanedausa/news/2912051600322116180?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

The numbered novels contain:

-   the actual progression of Miyagi and Sendai's relationship;

-   the alternating interior narration;

-   major changes in physical intimacy;

-   graduation;

-   university;

-   cohabitation;

-   jealousy;

-   boundaries;

-   evolving domesticity;

-   and the principal development of the supporting cast.


And crucially, **the books themselves already contain newly written material**. The retailer SS are *additional* 書き下ろし, not pieces removed from the main novel and sold separately.

So if we read Volumes 1–9 without acquiring another commercial extra, I would still be comfortable eventually writing an **authoritative LN-series synthesis through Volume 9**.

I would not attach a scientific percentage to this, but as an analytical heuristic I'd say we retain something like **well over 90% of the material necessary to understand the central literary work**. That is a judgment about analytical value, not a measured percentage of word count or canon.

## Miyagi and Sendai: this is where the loss matters more

The bonuses are disproportionately valuable for *週クラ* because its characterization occurs through tiny things.

A four-page story about:

-   shampoo;

-   hand cream;

-   cake;

-   what one girl eats;

-   waiting for the other to come home;

-   going somewhere together in the morning;

-   or an apparently meaningless ordinary day


can tell us quite a lot about them.

In a plot-heavy fantasy series, these might genuinely be disposable bonuses. In *週クラ*, ordinary behavior **is one of the primary character texts**.

Volume 8 makes this particularly clear. Haneda says its retailer SS are connected to episodes in the book, and both the Melonbooks and BOOK☆WALKER stories contain **both Miyagi and Sendai viewpoints**. The paid 「宮城と印、仙台さんと印」 is a 26-page two-perspective novella. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)カクヨム - 「書ける、読める、伝えられる」新しいWeb小説サイト+1](https://kakuyomu.jp/users/hanedausa/news/822139840843116397?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

That's analytically attractive because alternating perspective is one of the things we care about most.

Suppose an LN scene leaves us thinking:

> Miyagi interprets this as possession; what did Sendai actually think?

A retailer SS may answer that from the other side.

So I'd call the absence **low-to-moderate loss for their psychological portrait**, especially in the university/cohabitation period.

It won't change:

> Miyagi is X and Sendai is Y.

It may refine:

> Sendai is more aware of X at this stage than I thought, while Miyagi is misreading Y for a very particular reason.

Those refinements are exactly the sort of thing that makes a final character deep dive better.

## Side characters: the free Kakuyomu stories matter surprisingly much

This is where we can recover a lot at no cost.

The twelve publicly readable stories disproportionately foreground people like:

-   **Utsunomiya Maika**

-   **Shirakawa Ami**

-   **Komatsu Mio**


rather than simply giving us more Miyagi/Sendai date material.

That means I'd actually consider the free Kakuyomu SS **higher priority for the project than collecting every tiny retailer bonus**.

The LN is overwhelmingly a two-person consciousness chamber. Supplemental stories can answer:

> What do these two look like from outside?

That's extremely valuable.

Maika in particular can tell us what Miyagi and Sendai's increasingly bizarre “we are absolutely not dating” relationship looks like to a reasonably socially competent third party.

So skipping the public SS would leave a larger hole in our **supporting-character and external-perspective analysis** than skipping one randomly selected retailer story.

## Voice and ordinary social rhythm: modest loss

For a work this dialogue-sensitive, more scenes improve our understanding of:

-   sentence endings;

-   who initiates topics;

-   teasing styles;

-   address forms;

-   silence;

-   how Miyagi phrases a refusal;

-   how Sendai disguises a request;

-   how differently each thinks versus speaks.


But nine full Japanese novels already give us an enormous sample. Bonus SS improve confidence; they aren't required to establish voice.

I'd call this **incremental rather than foundational**.

## Erotic/physical relationship analysis: some bonuses could matter

Given our earlier discussion of Sendai as the more naturally proactive partner, this is one area where I'd be reluctant to say the extras are entirely negligible.

A bonus story involving:

-   marks;

-   bathing;

-   clothing;

-   sleeping arrangements;

-   physical proximity;

-   jealousy;

-   or bodily care


might supply another clean example of who initiates, who permits, who escalates, and who retreats.

The Volume 8 paid novella **「宮城と印、仙台さんと印」** immediately looks high-priority on title, length, dual perspective, and placement alone. I'm deliberately not inferring its actual contents beyond what the author publicly says; we need the text before drawing conclusions. But if I were prioritizing scarce extras for acquisition, that would be near the top. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)カクヨム - 「書ける、読める、伝えられる」新しいWeb小説サイト](https://kakuyomu.jp/users/hanedausa/news/822139840843116397?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Similarly, Volume 9's paid **「仙台さんの夢、宮城の夢」** contains both perspectives and is larger than an ordinary four-page retailer SS. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)カクヨム - 「書ける、読める、伝えられる」新しいWeb小説サイト](https://kakuyomu.jp/users/hanedausa/news/2912051599398591741?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Those are much more interesting analytically than trying to obtain every tiny shop bonus merely for completeness.

---

# I would rank the missing material this way

**Tier 1 — Read before final synthesis if obtainable**

The twelve full Kakuyomu commemorative SS, because they're freely available and often expand supporting perspectives; your existing BOOK☆WALKER stories; complete author-written voice dramas; the substantial 26-page dual-POV Volume 8 and Volume 9 paid novellas if we can eventually obtain them.

**Tier 2 — Worth incorporating, but don't delay the main analysis for them**

The missing BOOK☆WALKER V2/V4/V5/V8 stories and the more substantial paired-perspective retailer stories. Volume 8 bonuses are somewhat more interesting because Haneda explicitly says they're linked to episodes in the novel. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://kakuyomu.jp&sz=128)カクヨム - 「書ける、読める、伝えられる」新しいWeb小説サイト](https://kakuyomu.jp/users/hanedausa/news/822139842400686823?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

**Tier 3 — Completionist**

Most four-page retailer SS, fair booklets, minor manga bonuses, and similar material. Useful for characterization and occasional surprises, but I would not hold up a Volume 1–9 synthesis waiting for them.

That distinction matters because there are enough retailer permutations that **chasing “100% Shuukura canon” could become a collection project rather than a literary-analysis project**.

---

## What I would do with the corpus we have

I think the strongest workflow is:

> **LN 1 → supplements → LN 2 → supplements → LN 3 → supplements → LN 4 → supplements → High-School Synthesis → LN 5–9 with supplements → Through-V9 Synthesis → WN continuation/comparison.**

For each numbered volume, I would make the **LN itself the source of all major conclusions**. Bonus material would be marked as refinement:

> “The Volume 8 retailer SS further clarifies…”

rather than allowed to carry an interpretation that the main novels do not support.

And after Volume 4, I strongly recommend stopping for a substantial synthesis before entering Volume 5. That isn't merely a convenient division. The transition from **weekly purchased classmate in Miyagi's room** to **post-high-school domestic life under one roof** changes the fundamental experimental conditions of the relationship. Analyzing what the ¥5,000 arrangement accomplished before seeing what happens when that structure disappears should produce a much cleaner reading.

So I would **start the actual deep read now rather than wait for the missing bonuses**. The material we already possess is more than adequate. The remaining extras are best thought of as high-resolution lenses we'll add where available, not missing chapters in the skeleton of the story.

-   `限定ボイスドラマ・試聴版` — about **1:46**

-   `７巻発売記念！ 書き下ろしボイスドラマ` — about **4:26**


I can transcribe the spoken Japanese, separate Miyagi/Shiori (Lynn) and Sendai/Hazuki (Kana Ichinose) where the voices are distinguishable, preserve nonverbal vocalizations when they matter, and produce timestamps. I can also note uncertain words rather than silently guessing.

One correction to your guess: based on the filenames, the middle file is not a general trailer. It is explicitly a **限定ボイスドラマ・試聴版**—a *preview/sample of a limited voice drama*. The other two are labeled **書き下ろしボイスドラマ**, meaning newly written voice dramas, with the third specifically commemorating Volume 7.

For this project, I’d recommend transcribing them in a format like:

`[00:12.4] 宮城: ……`
`[00:15.8] 仙台: ……`
`[00:18.1] 宮城: （ため息）……`

Then I can add a second layer with:

-   literal English gloss where useful,

-   character-voice/register observations,

-   pauses, sighs, laughs, huffs, hesitations, etc.,

-   and notes on anything the delivery adds beyond the written words.


These are especially worthwhile for *週クラ*, because vocal initiative, hesitation, teasing, and emotional masking are central to Miyagi/Sendai characterization.
